Hi, yes, thank you for seeing me. Um, well, I guess I'm here because... things have just gotten really frustrating lately with my eating habits, and I didn't really know who else to talk to. I'm a graphic designer, and I've been taking on a lot of intensive projects lately, which means my schedule is kind of all over the place. I end up working from home a lot, pushing up against these really tight deadlines, staring at my screens for hours on end. 

And what brings me in is that on these late work nights, I’ll open up a delivery app and order what I tell myself is just going to be a normal dinner. You know, just a regular meal because I'm tired and I've been working hard. But then... it doesn't stay just dinner. It turns into this huge thing. I'll order a whole entrée, but then I'll add on multiple sides, and a dessert, and then even with all of that, I'll still end up getting up and grabbing extra snacks from my own kitchen pantry. It just turns into this massive amount of food. It's way, way more than I’d ever eat if someone were sitting across from me, like if I were out at a restaurant with a friend or a date. But because I'm alone in my apartment, it just spirals into this huge pile of food, and it's really starting to bother me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it’s like... okay, so usually the food arrives, right? And I’ll bring it all inside and set it up on my desk, or maybe the coffee table if my back is hurting and I've moved my laptop to the couch. And I tell myself I’m just going to eat the main dish. Like, I’ll start with whatever the actual dinner part is. And it’s fine at first, I'm just eating because I'm hungry and I've been working for ten hours straight. 

But then... I don't know, it’s like this weird shift happens. Once I start, there’s this specific point where... I don't even know how to describe it properly, but it's almost like I'm sitting outside of my own body? I’m just watching myself do it. I'll finish the meal, and then I'm immediately reaching for the extra sides, and my brain is going, "You're full, you don't need this, you should stop," but my hands are just opening the containers anyway. I’m literally watching myself keep going back to the food, opening another package, or ripping open a bag of cookies from the pantry, and I’m thinking so clearly in my head that I really, really should stop. But it just feels like the decision has already slipped completely out of my hands. 

It’s like I’m on autopilot. And it’s so incredibly frustrating because, you know, I'm a capable person! I manage these massive design campaigns with a million moving parts, I can organize and control my work perfectly fine. But in that moment, sitting in my living room, the logic just doesn't matter. I'll just keep eating, opening wrapper after wrapper, even when I don't even really want it anymore or the food has gotten cold. I just can't seem to hit the brakes once that momentum starts, no matter how much I'm begging myself internally to just put the food down. It's just... it's like I have no say in the matter anymore once that line is crossed.

Yeah, exactly. I mean, that is exactly what I was just trying to explain. It is a complete and total loss of control. I really can't stop once I start. I will be sitting there, and my brain is screaming at me to just close the takeout box, or put the bag of cookies away, or whatever it is I am eating. And I just... don't. I can't. 

It is so bizarre because in every other aspect of my life, like with my design projects, if a client tells me to stop and pivot, I pivot. I have complete control over my schedule, my emails, my layouts. I am a professional! But with this... it is like a switch flips in my brain and the wiring just completely shorts out. 

I remember this one time just last week, I was working on a branding package for this organic skincare line, which is kind of ironic, right, because I am sitting there eating absolute junk while designing for wellness, but anyway, I kept telling myself that I would take just one more bite and then I would be done, because I needed to finish this logo for a morning presentation. And I just couldn't do it. I literally could not make my hands stop bringing the food to my mouth. It is terrifying, honestly, to feel like your own body isn't listening to you. 

So, yes, it is exactly that. I feel completely out of control, like the brakes are completely cut and I am just watching the car crash, you know? I just sit there and watch myself do it, and I can't do a single thing to stop it until the food is just... gone.

And I just couldn't do it. I literally could not make my hands stop bringing the food to my mouth. It is terrifying, honestly, to feel like your own body isn't listening to you. \n\nSo, yes, it is exactly that. I feel completely out of control, like the brakes are completely cut and I am just watching the car crash, you know? Distress? I mean... yeah, it definitely causes distress. I think 'distress' is probably putting it mildly, honestly. Like I was saying, it’s just so incredibly frustrating. But as for it actually interfering with my daily life... I don't know, I'm not really sure I'd say it interferes with things like my work or my relationships in a practical way? 

Like, I still hit all my deadlines. I mentioned that skincare presentation earlier—I still finished the logo that night, I got up the next morning, I gave the presentation on Zoom, and the client absolutely loved it. So my work is totally fine. I am very disciplined when it comes to my career, I always push through and get the job done. And as for relationships... well, I work from home and I've been so swamped with these massive design campaigns lately that I'm not really seeing anyone right now anyway. I just don't have the time or energy to date or go out to dinners with friends, so there isn't really a relationship for this to interfere *with*, if that makes sense? My schedule is just work, work, work right now. 

But... how I see myself? Yeah. That is where it really gets to me. It's this heavy, awful, sinking feeling, especially the next morning. I'll wake up and walk into the living room, and I'll see the empty takeout containers still sitting on my coffee table, or the cookie wrappers stuffed into the top of the trash can, and I just feel so... embarrassed. I'll just stand there making my morning coffee, staring at the mess I made, and I will just beat myself up over it for hours. I'll be thinking, 'You're thirty-three years old, you manage million-dollar campaigns, you have a degree, why can't you just eat a normal dinner like a normal, functioning adult?' 

So it's not like I'm missing work or blowing off social events, because, like I said, I'm doing this alone in my apartment late at night. But it's this internal monologue that just completely tears me down. I spend so much mental energy just being mad at myself and feeling ashamed. And then I have to put on this professional, smiling face for my client calls and act like I have my entire life perfectly put together, when really I'm just so disappointed in myself for what I did the night before. So I don't know if that's exactly what you mean by interfering, because I'm still functioning on paper, but the distress is definitely there... it's just all trapped inside my own head.

I don't even know how to describe it properly, but it's almost like I'm sitting outside of my own body? I’m just watching myself do it. I'll finish the meal, and then I'm immediately reaching for the extra sides, and my brain is going, \"You're full, you don't need this, you should stop,\" but my hands are just opening the containers anyway. I’m literally watching myself keep going back to the food, opening another package, or ripping open a bag of cookies from the pantry, and I’m thinking so clearly in my head that I really, really should stop. But it just feels like the decision has already slipped completely out of my hands. \n\nIt’s like I’m on autopilot. And it’s so incredibly frustrating because, you know, I'm a capable person! I manage these massive design campaigns with a million moving parts, I can organize and control my work perfectly fine. But in that moment, sitting in my living room, the logic just doesn't matter. I'll just keep eating, opening wrapper after wrapper, even when I don't even really want it anymore or the food has gotten cold. I just can't seem to hit the brakes once that momentum starts, no matter how much I'm begging myself internally to just put the food down. It's just... it's like I have no say in the matter anymore once that line is crossed.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're not in control of your eating, like you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I mean, that is exactly what I was just trying to explain. It is a complete and total loss of control. I really can't stop once I start. I will be sitting there, and my brain is screaming at me to just close the takeout box, or put the bag of cookies away, or whatever it is I am eating. And I just... don't. I can't. \n\nIt is so bizarre because in every other aspect of my life, like with my design projects, if a client tells me to stop and pivot, I pivot. I have complete control over my schedule, my emails, my layouts. I am a professional! But with this... it is like a switch flips in my brain and the wiring just completely shorts out. \n\nI remember this one time just last week, I was working on a branding package for this organic skincare line, which is kind of ironic, right, because I am sitting there eating absolute junk while designing for wellness, but anyway, I kept telling myself that I would take just one more bite and then I would be done, because I needed to finish this logo for a morning presentation. And I just couldn't do it. I literally could not make my hands stop bringing the food to my mouth. It is terrifying, honestly, to feel like your own body isn't listening to you. \n\nSo, yes, it is exactly that. I feel completely out of control, like the brakes are completely cut and I am just watching the car crash, you know? I just sit there and watch myself do it, and I can't do a single thing to stop it until the food is just... gone.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life, such as affecting your work, relationships, or how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Distress? I mean... yeah, it definitely causes distress. I think 'distress' is probably putting it mildly, honestly. Like I was saying, it’s just so incredibly frustrating. But as for it actually interfering with my daily life... I don't know, I'm not really sure I'd say it interferes with things like my work or my relationships in a practical way? \n\nLike, I still hit all my deadlines. I mentioned that skincare presentation earlier—I still finished the logo that night, I got up the next morning, I gave the presentation on Zoom, and the client absolutely loved it. So my work is totally fine. I am very disciplined when it comes to my career, I always push through and get the job done. And as for relationships... well, I work from home and I've been so swamped with these massive design campaigns lately that I'm not really seeing anyone right now anyway. I just don't have the time or energy to date or go out to dinners with friends, so there isn't really a relationship for this to interfere *with*, if that makes sense? My schedule is just work, work, work right now. \n\nBut... how I see myself? Yeah. That is where it really gets to me. It's this heavy, awful, sinking feeling, especially the next morning. I'll wake up and walk into the living room, and I'll see the empty takeout containers still sitting on my coffee table, or the cookie wrappers stuffed into the top of the trash can, and I just feel so... embarrassed. I'll just stand there making my morning coffee, staring at the mess I made, and I will just beat myself up over it for hours. I'll be thinking, 'You're thirty-three years old, you manage million-dollar campaigns, you have a degree, why can't you just eat a normal dinner like a normal, functioning adult?' \n\nSo it's not like I'm missing work or blowing off social events, because, like I said, I'm doing this alone in my apartment late at night. But it's this internal monologue that just completely tears me down. I spend so much mental energy just being mad at myself and feeling ashamed. And then I have to put on this professional, smiling face for my client calls and act like I have my entire life perfectly put together, when really I'm just so disappointed in myself for what I did the night before. So I don't know if that's exactly what you mean by interfering, because I'm still functioning on paper, but the distress is definitely there... it's just all trapped inside my own head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

So my work is totally fine. I am very disciplined when it comes to my career, I always push through and get the job done. And as for relationships... well, I work from home and I've been so swamped with these massive design campaigns lately that I'm not really seeing anyone right now anyway. I just don't have the time or energy to date or go out to dinners with friends, so there isn't really a relationship for this to interfere *with*, if that makes sense? My schedule is just work, work, work right now. \n\nBut... how I see myself? Yeah. That is where it really gets to me. It's this heavy, awful, sinking feeling, especially the next morning. I'll wake up and walk into the living room, and I'll see the empty takeout containers still sitting on my coffee table, or the cookie wrappers stuffed into the top of the trash can, and I just feel so... embarrassed. I'll just stand there making my morning coffee, staring at the mess I made, and I will just beat myself up over it for hours. I'll be thinking, 'You're thirty-three years old, you manage million-dollar campaigns, you have a degree, why can't you just eat a normal dinner like a normal, functioning adult?' \n\nSo it's not like I'm missing work or blowing off social events, because, like I said, I'm doing this alone in my apartment late at night. But it's this internal monologue that just completely tears me down. I spend so much mental energy just being mad at myself and feeling ashamed. And then I have to put on this professional, smiling face for my client calls and act like I have my entire life perfectly put together, when really I'm just so disappointed in myself for what I did the night before. So I don't know if that's exactly what you mean by interfering, because I'm still functioning on paper, but the distress is definitely there... it's just all trapped inside my own head.\n\n[Last Question]\nAfter these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to try to compensate or make up for it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / graphic_designer / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I don't do any of that. I mean... I know some people do, you hear about that sort of thing on TV or whatever, but I've never made myself throw up. The very thought of it honestly just makes me shudder. I absolutely hate throwing up—even when I'm genuinely sick, like if I have a stomach bug, I will fight it for hours. So doing it on purpose? No way, I could never bring myself to do that. 

And laxatives, definitely not. I wouldn't even know where to start with that. Plus, honestly, my stomach is already in absolute knots the next day just from all the rich takeout and the sugar I ate, so adding something like a laxative into the mix just sounds like a complete nightmare. Especially with my work schedule! I have these morning Zoom calls with clients, and I have to be completely focused when I'm presenting a mock-up or a branding strategy. I can't be worried about running to the bathroom in the middle of pitching a design concept to a CEO. 

As for exercise... I mean, I honestly wish I exercised normally, let alone excessively. But no, definitely not. Like I was saying earlier, I sit at my desk for ten, sometimes twelve hours a day, or I'm hunched over my laptop on the couch because my lower back is just completely shot from my terrible posture. By the time one of these eating things is over, I am just so physically exhausted and uncomfortably full. Like, painfully full, where your clothes feel too tight. The idea of putting on running shoes and trying to go for a jog at midnight? It's just not happening. I can barely muster the energy to waddle to the kitchen to throw the wrappers away. 

Usually, I just kind of sit there in this horrible food coma, feeling absolutely miserable and heavy. I don't try to undo it or burn it off. I just eventually drag myself to bed and just... lay there. I just sit with the guilt of it, honestly. It's just me, a terrible stomach ache, and a whole lot of regret. So, yeah, to answer your question, no, I don't do any of those things to make up for it. I just kind of suffer through the aftermath the next day.

physically hungry at all but I’m still wandering into the kitchen or ordering delivery while I’m trying to finish a project.\n- key: S054\n  name: Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment\n  description: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n  manifestation: I’m careful to eat lightly if I’m with coworkers or friends, but when I’m alone in my condo after midnight I’ll close the blinds and get rid of the delivery containers quickly because I’d be mortified if anyone saw how much there was.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating occurs, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: When I look back at my delivery receipts and calendar, there’s usually at least one night every about ten months, since shortly after I closed on the condo and started taking on all the extra freelance work.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / graphic_designer / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S049\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're not in control of your eating, like you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I mean, that is exactly what I was just trying to explain. It is a complete and total loss of control. I really can't stop once I start. I will be sitting there, and my brain is screaming at me to just close the takeout box, or put the bag of cookies away, or whatever it is I am eating. And I just... don't. I can't. \n\nIt is so bizarre because in every other aspect of my life, like with my design projects, if a client tells me to stop and pivot, I pivot. I have complete control over my schedule, my emails, my layouts. I am a professional! But with this... it is like a switch flips in my brain and the wiring just completely shorts out. \n\nI remember this one time just last week, I was working on a branding package for this organic skincare line, which is kind of ironic, right, because I am sitting there eating absolute junk while designing for wellness, but anyway, I kept telling myself that I would take just one more bite and then I would be done, because I needed to finish this logo for a morning presentation. And I just couldn't do it. I literally could not make my hands stop bringing the food to my mouth. It is terrifying, honestly, to feel like your own body isn't listening to you. \n\nSo, yes, it is exactly that. I feel completely out of control, like the brakes are completely cut and I am just watching the car crash, you know? I just sit there and watch myself do it, and I can't do a single thing to stop it until the food is just... gone.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life, such as affecting your work, relationships, or how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Distress? I mean... yeah, it definitely causes distress. I think 'distress' is probably putting it mildly, honestly. Like I was saying, it’s just so incredibly frustrating. But as for it actually interfering with my daily life... I don't know, I'm not really sure I'd say it interferes with things like my work or my relationships in a practical way? \n\nLike, I still hit all my deadlines. I mentioned that skincare presentation earlier—I still finished the logo that night, I got up the next morning, I gave the presentation on Zoom, and the client absolutely loved it. So my work is totally fine. I am very disciplined when it comes to my career, I always push through and get the job done. And as for relationships... well, I work from home and I've been so swamped with these massive design campaigns lately that I'm not really seeing anyone right now anyway. I just don't have the time or energy to date or go out to dinners with friends, so there isn't really a relationship for this to interfere *with*, if that makes sense? My schedule is just work, work, work right now. \n\nBut... how I see myself? Yeah. That is where it really gets to me. It's this heavy, awful, sinking feeling, especially the next morning. I'll wake up and walk into the living room, and I'll see the empty takeout containers still sitting on my coffee table, or the cookie wrappers stuffed into the top of the trash can, and I just feel so... embarrassed. I'll just stand there making my morning coffee, staring at the mess I made, and I will just beat myself up over it for hours. I'll be thinking, 'You're thirty-three years old, you manage million-dollar campaigns, you have a degree, why can't you just eat a normal dinner like a normal, functioning adult?' \n\nSo it's not like I'm missing work or blowing off social events, because, like I said, I'm doing this alone in my apartment late at night. But it's this internal monologue that just completely tears me down. I spend so much mental energy just being mad at myself and feeling ashamed. And then I have to put on this professional, smiling face for my client calls and act like I have my entire life perfectly put together, when really I'm just so disappointed in myself for what I did the night before. So I don't know if that's exactly what you mean by interfering, because I'm still functioning on paper, but the distress is definitely there... it's just all trapped inside my own head.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to try to compensate or make up for it?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do any of that. I mean... I know some people do, you hear about that sort of thing on TV or whatever, but I've never made myself throw up. The very thought of it honestly just makes me shudder. I absolutely hate throwing up—even when I'm genuinely sick, like if I have a stomach bug, I will fight it for hours. So doing it on purpose? No way, I could never bring myself to do that. \n\nAnd laxatives, definitely not. I wouldn't even know where to start with that. Plus, honestly, my stomach is already in absolute knots the next day just from all the rich takeout and the sugar I ate, so adding something like a laxative into the mix just sounds like a complete nightmare. Especially with my work schedule! I have these morning Zoom calls with clients, and I have to be completely focused when I'm presenting a mock-up or a branding strategy. I can't be worried about running to the bathroom in the middle of pitching a design concept to a CEO. \n\nAs for exercise... I mean, I honestly wish I exercised normally, let alone excessively. But no, definitely not. Like I was saying earlier, I sit at my desk for ten, sometimes twelve hours a day, or I'm hunched over my laptop on the couch because my lower back is just completely shot from my terrible posture. By the time one of these eating things is over, I am just so physically exhausted and uncomfortably full. Like, painfully full, where your clothes feel too tight. The idea of putting on running shoes and trying to go for a jog at midnight? It's just not happening. I can barely muster the energy to waddle to the kitchen to throw the wrappers away. \n\nUsually, I just kind of sit there in this horrible food coma, feeling absolutely miserable and heavy. I don't try to undo it or burn it off. I just eventually drag myself to bed and just... lay there. I just sit with the guilt of it, honestly. It's just me, a terrible stomach ache, and a whole lot of regret. So, yeah, to answer your question, no, I don't do any of those things to make up for it. I just kind of suffer through the aftermath the next day.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring these episodes, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, definitely. It's actually kind of shocking how fast it happens. I mean, I'll be sitting right there at my desk, right? Usually I have my dual monitors going, and I'll have all these Adobe Illustrator files open—maybe I'm tweaking a vector graphic or adjusting the kerning on a font for a logo, something really tedious where I'm just staring intently at the screen—and I'll have the food spread out all around my keyboard. 

And once I start, it's like my hands are just moving on complete autopilot. I eat so, so fast. It's not like a normal dinner where you take a bite, chew, take a sip of water, actually think about the flavor... no, I barely even notice the taste at all. I'm just blindly reaching and moving from one takeout container to the next. Like, from the carton of noodles to the dumplings to whatever else is sitting there, just rotating through them. 

And I'm just shoveling it in while staring at my screen. It's almost like a trance, you know? My eyes are glued to the Illustrator canvas, and my hand is just going back and forth, back and forth from the desk to my mouth. And then, I don't know, maybe just a few minutes will go by—literally just a handful of minutes—and I'll reach down for another bite and my fork will just scrape the bottom of the plastic container. And I'll look down, pull myself out of the screen for a second, and realize I've already swallowed almost all of it. 

It's just... gone. A massive amount of food, just gone in a flash. It's honestly crazy how quickly I can put away that much food without even registering the physical experience of eating it. I don't savor it, I don't enjoy it, I just completely inhale it.

It's honestly crazy how quickly I can put away that much food without even registering the physical experience of eating it. I don't know, I'm not really sure I'd say it interferes with things like my work or my relationships in a practical way? \n\nLike, I still hit all my deadlines. So my work is totally fine. I am very disciplined when it comes to my career, I always push through and get the job done. And as for relationships... well, I work from home and I've been so swamped with these massive design campaigns lately that I'm not really seeing anyone right now anyway. I just don't have the time or energy to date or go out to dinners with friends, so there isn't really a relationship for this to interfere *with*, if that makes sense? My schedule is just work, work, work right now. \n\nBut... how I see myself? Yeah. That is where it really gets to me. It's this heavy, awful, sinking feeling, especially the next morning. I'll wake up and walk into the living room, and I'll see the empty takeout containers still sitting on my coffee table, or the cookie wrappers stuffed into the top of the trash can, and I just feel so... embarrassed. I'll just stand there making my morning coffee, staring at the mess I made, and I will just beat myself up over it for hours. I'll be thinking, 'You're thirty-three years old, you manage million-dollar campaigns, you have a degree, why can't you just eat a normal dinner like a normal, functioning adult?' \n\nSo it's not like I'm missing work or blowing off social events, because, like I said, I'm doing this alone in my apartment late at night. I absolutely hate throwing up—even when I'm genuinely sick, like if I have a stomach bug, I will fight it for hours. So doing it on purpose? I just kind of suffer through the aftermath the next day.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. It's actually kind of shocking how fast it happens. I mean, I'll be sitting right there at my desk, right? Usually I have my dual monitors going, and I'll have all these Adobe Illustrator files open—maybe I'm tweaking a vector graphic or adjusting the kerning on a font for a logo, something really tedious where I'm just staring intently at the screen—and I'll have the food spread out all around my keyboard. \n\nAnd once I start, it's like my hands are just moving on complete autopilot. I eat so, so fast. It's not like a normal dinner where you take a bite, chew, take a sip of water, actually think about the flavor... no, I barely even notice the taste at all. I'm just blindly reaching and moving from one takeout container to the next. Like, from the carton of noodles to the dumplings to whatever else is sitting there, just rotating through them. \n\nAnd I'm just shoveling it in while staring at my screen. It's almost like a trance, you know? My eyes are glued to the Illustrator canvas, and my hand is just going back and forth, back and forth from the desk to my mouth. And then, I don't know, maybe just a few minutes will go by—literally just a handful of minutes—and I'll reach down for another bite and my fork will just scrape the bottom of the plastic container. And I'll look down, pull myself out of the screen for a second, and realize I've already swallowed almost all of it. \n\nIt's just... gone. A massive amount of food, just gone in a flash. It's honestly crazy how quickly I can put away that much food without even registering the physical experience of eating it. I don't savor it, I don't enjoy it, I just completely inhale it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow often do these episodes happen? Like, how many times a week or month would you say this occurs?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / graphic_designer / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"When I look back at my delivery receipts and calendar, there’s usually at least one night every week where this happens, sometimes more, and that pattern has been there for several months.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's funny you ask that, because I actually... I was looking at my phone just the other day, scrolling through my UberEats and DoorDash history. You know how those apps keep a record of literally everything? I was trying to figure out my budget, because honestly, the delivery fees and the service charges alone are just outrageous right now, and I felt like I was spending way too much money. 

And when I was looking back at all those delivery receipts, and kind of cross-referencing them with my work calendar—because my calendar is just this endless wall of deadlines and Zoom links—I noticed this really obvious, kind of terrifying pattern. There is usually at least one night every single week where an episode like this happens. Like, without fail. Sometimes it's more, maybe two times a week if I'm dealing with a particularly demanding client or if I have to do a massive revision on a logo design at the last minute and I'm up late. But definitely at least once a week. 

And as I kept scrolling back, and back... I realized this pattern has been there for several months now. I'd say it really started picking up when I took on this huge new corporate account a while back. The months just completely blur together when you work from home and stare at Adobe Illustrator all day, so I hadn't even realized how long it had been going on. 

But yeah, seeing it all laid out there in my order history, all those late-night massive orders, week after week for months... it was a real wake-up call. Because when you're in the middle of it, you just tell yourself, 'Oh, it's just tonight, I'm just stressed today, it's a one-off.' But then you look at the app and it's every single week for months on end. You just don't realize how frequent it is until the data is literally just staring you right in the face, you know? It's just hard to look at.

I'll just keep eating, opening wrapper after wrapper, even when I don't even really want it anymore or the food has gotten cold. I can't. \n\nIt is so bizarre because in every other aspect of my life, like with my design projects, if a client tells me to stop and pivot, I pivot. I mean... yeah, it definitely causes distress. I think 'distress' is probably putting it mildly, honestly. Like I was saying, it’s just so incredibly frustrating. But as for it actually interfering with my daily life... I don't know, I'm not really sure I'd say it interferes with things like my work or my relationships in a practical way? \n\nLike, I still hit all my deadlines. I mentioned that skincare presentation earlier—I still finished the logo that night, I got up the next morning, I gave the presentation on Zoom, and the client absolutely loved it. So my work is totally fine. I am very disciplined when it comes to my career, I always push through and get the job done. And as for relationships... well, I work from home and I've been so swamped with these massive design campaigns lately that I'm not really seeing anyone right now anyway. I just don't have the time or energy to date or go out to dinners with friends, so there isn't really a relationship for this to interfere *with*, if that makes sense? My schedule is just work, work, work right now. \n\nBut... how I see myself? Yeah. That is where it really gets to me. It's this heavy, awful, sinking feeling, especially the next morning. I'll wake up and walk into the living room, and I'll see the empty takeout containers still sitting on my coffee table, or the cookie wrappers stuffed into the top of the trash can, and I just feel so... embarrassed. I'll just stand there making my morning coffee, staring at the mess I made, and I will just beat myself up over it for hours. I'll be thinking, 'You're thirty-three years old, you manage million-dollar campaigns, you have a degree, why can't you just eat a normal dinner like a normal, functioning adult?' \n\nSo it's not like I'm missing work or blowing off social events, because, like I said, I'm doing this alone in my apartment late at night. But it's this internal monologue that just completely tears me down. I spend so much mental energy just being mad at myself and feeling ashamed. And then I have to put on this professional, smiling face for my client calls and act like I have my entire life perfectly put together, when really I'm just so disappointed in myself for what I did the night before. So I don't know if that's exactly what you mean by interfering, because I'm still functioning on paper, but the distress is definitely there... it's just all trapped inside my own head.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to try to compensate or make up for it?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do any of that. I mean... I know some people do, you hear about that sort of thing on TV or whatever, but I've never made myself throw up. The very thought of it honestly just makes me shudder. I absolutely hate throwing up—even when I'm genuinely sick, like if I have a stomach bug, I will fight it for hours. So doing it on purpose? No way, I could never bring myself to do that. \n\nAnd laxatives, definitely not. I wouldn't even know where to start with that. Plus, honestly, my stomach is already in absolute knots the next day just from all the rich takeout and the sugar I ate, so adding something like a laxative into the mix just sounds like a complete nightmare. Especially with my work schedule! I have these morning Zoom calls with clients, and I have to be completely focused when I'm presenting a mock-up or a branding strategy. By the time one of these eating things is over, I am just so physically exhausted and uncomfortably full. Like, painfully full, where your clothes feel too tight. The idea of putting on running shoes and trying to go for a jog at midnight? It's just not happening. I eat so, so fast. It's not like a normal dinner where you take a bite, chew, take a sip of water, actually think about the flavor... no, I barely even notice the taste at all. I'm just blindly reaching and moving from one takeout container to the next. And I'll look down, pull myself out of the screen for a second, and realize I've already swallowed almost all of it. \n\nIt's just... gone. A massive amount of food, just gone in a flash. It's honestly crazy how quickly I can put away that much food without even registering the physical experience of eating it. I don't savor it, I don't enjoy it, I just completely inhale it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes happen? I was trying to figure out my budget, because honestly, the delivery fees and the service charges alone are just outrageous right now, and I felt like I was spending way too much money. \n\nAnd when I was looking back at all those delivery receipts, and kind of cross-referencing them with my work calendar—because my calendar is just this endless wall of deadlines and Zoom links—I noticed this really obvious, kind of terrifying pattern. There is usually at least one night every single week where an episode like this happens. Like, without fail. Sometimes it's more, maybe two times a week if I'm dealing with a particularly demanding client or if I have to do a massive revision on a logo design at the last minute and I'm up late. But definitely at least once a week. \n\nAnd as I kept scrolling back, and back... I realized this pattern has been there for several months now. I'd say it really started picking up when I took on this huge new corporate account a while back. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, God, yes. It goes so far past just being full. I mean, normal fullness is when you finish a plate of food and think, 'Okay, that was good, I'm satisfied.' But this is completely different. I just keep going and going. Even when my brain is vaguely registering that I'm not hungry anymore, or that I've already had way too much, I just can't seem to stop until the containers are completely empty. 

And the physical sensation by the end of it... it's just awful. Like I mentioned before about feeling miserably heavy, it's honestly to the point where I am painfully, uncomfortably stuffed. By the end, I'm often so full that my stomach feels incredibly tight against my waistband. And I work from home, so I'm usually just wearing sweatpants or soft leggings, but even those elastic waistbands feel like they're cutting right into my skin because my stomach is just so distended from all the food. 

It actually physically hurts. It gets so bad that I have to just sort of migrate over to the couch, and I literally have to sit very, very still. If I twist or bend or move too quickly, it's just this sharp, uncomfortable pressure, almost like I can't even take a full, deep breath. So I just stay frozen there on the cushions, sometimes for an hour or more, just waiting for the worst of that agonizing fullness to pass before I can even bend over to clean up the takeout bags and the mess off my desk. It's just a miserable, miserable physical feeling, but during the actual eating part, I just keep pushing right past it without even stopping to realize how much it's going to hurt.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yes, thank you for seeing me. Um, well, I guess I'm here because... things have just gotten really frustrating lately with my eating habits, and I didn't really know who else to talk to. I'm a graphic designer, and I've been taking on a lot of intensive projects lately, which means my schedule is kind of all over the place. I end up working from home a lot, pushing up against these really tight deadlines, staring at my screens for hours on end. \n\nAnd what brings me in is that on these late work nights, I’ll open up a delivery app and order what I tell myself is just going to be a normal dinner. You know, just a regular meal because I'm tired and I've been working hard. But then... it doesn't stay just dinner. It turns into this huge thing. I'll order a whole entrée, but then I'll add on multiple sides, and a dessert, and then even with all of that, I'll still end up getting up and grabbing extra snacks from my own kitchen pantry. It just turns into this massive amount of food. It's way, way more than I’d ever eat if someone were sitting across from me, like if I were out at a restaurant with a friend or a date. But because I'm alone in my apartment, it just spirals into this huge pile of food, and it's really starting to bother me.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what happens during these times when you end up eating a lot more than you originally planned? How do those episodes usually unfold for you?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s like... okay, so usually the food arrives, right? And I’ll bring it all inside and set it up on my desk, or maybe the coffee table if my back is hurting and I've moved my laptop to the couch. And I tell myself I’m just going to eat the main dish. Like, I’ll start with whatever the actual dinner part is. And it’s fine at first, I'm just eating because I'm hungry and I've been working for ten hours straight. \n\nBut then... I don't know, it’s like this weird shift happens. Once I start, there’s this specific point where... I don't even know how to describe it properly, but it's almost like I'm sitting outside of my own body? I’m just watching myself do it. I'll finish the meal, and then I'm immediately reaching for the extra sides, and my brain is going, \"You're full, you don't need this, you should stop,\" but my hands are just opening the containers anyway. I’m literally watching myself keep going back to the food, opening another package, or ripping open a bag of cookies from the pantry, and I’m thinking so clearly in my head that I really, really should stop. But it just feels like the decision has already slipped completely out of my hands. \n\nIt’s like I’m on autopilot. And it’s so incredibly frustrating because, you know, I'm a capable person! I manage these massive design campaigns with a million moving parts, I can organize and control my work perfectly fine. But in that moment, sitting in my living room, the logic just doesn't matter. I'll just keep eating, opening wrapper after wrapper, even when I don't even really want it anymore or the food has gotten cold. I just can't seem to hit the brakes once that momentum starts, no matter how much I'm begging myself internally to just put the food down. It's just... it's like I have no say in the matter anymore once that line is crossed.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're not in control of your eating, like you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I mean, that is exactly what I was just trying to explain. It is a complete and total loss of control. I really can't stop once I start. I will be sitting there, and my brain is screaming at me to just close the takeout box, or put the bag of cookies away, or whatever it is I am eating. And I just... don't. I can't. \n\nIt is so bizarre because in every other aspect of my life, like with my design projects, if a client tells me to stop and pivot, I pivot. I have complete control over my schedule, my emails, my layouts. I am a professional! But with this... it is like a switch flips in my brain and the wiring just completely shorts out. \n\nI remember this one time just last week, I was working on a branding package for this organic skincare line, which is kind of ironic, right, because I am sitting there eating absolute junk while designing for wellness, but anyway, I kept telling myself that I would take just one more bite and then I would be done, because I needed to finish this logo for a morning presentation. And I just couldn't do it. I literally could not make my hands stop bringing the food to my mouth. It is terrifying, honestly, to feel like your own body isn't listening to you. \n\nSo, yes, it is exactly that. I feel completely out of control, like the brakes are completely cut and I am just watching the car crash, you know? I just sit there and watch myself do it, and I can't do a single thing to stop it until the food is just... gone.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life, such as affecting your work, relationships, or how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Distress? I mean... yeah, it definitely causes distress. I think 'distress' is probably putting it mildly, honestly. Like I was saying, it’s just so incredibly frustrating. But as for it actually interfering with my daily life... I don't know, I'm not really sure I'd say it interferes with things like my work or my relationships in a practical way? \n\nLike, I still hit all my deadlines. I mentioned that skincare presentation earlier—I still finished the logo that night, I got up the next morning, I gave the presentation on Zoom, and the client absolutely loved it. So my work is totally fine. I am very disciplined when it comes to my career, I always push through and get the job done. And as for relationships... well, I work from home and I've been so swamped with these massive design campaigns lately that I'm not really seeing anyone right now anyway. I just don't have the time or energy to date or go out to dinners with friends, so there isn't really a relationship for this to interfere *with*, if that makes sense? My schedule is just work, work, work right now. \n\nBut... how I see myself? Yeah. That is where it really gets to me. It's this heavy, awful, sinking feeling, especially the next morning. I'll wake up and walk into the living room, and I'll see the empty takeout containers still sitting on my coffee table, or the cookie wrappers stuffed into the top of the trash can, and I just feel so... embarrassed. I'll just stand there making my morning coffee, staring at the mess I made, and I will just beat myself up over it for hours. I'll be thinking, 'You're thirty-three years old, you manage million-dollar campaigns, you have a degree, why can't you just eat a normal dinner like a normal, functioning adult?' \n\nSo it's not like I'm missing work or blowing off social events, because, like I said, I'm doing this alone in my apartment late at night. But it's this internal monologue that just completely tears me down. I spend so much mental energy just being mad at myself and feeling ashamed. And then I have to put on this professional, smiling face for my client calls and act like I have my entire life perfectly put together, when really I'm just so disappointed in myself for what I did the night before. So I don't know if that's exactly what you mean by interfering, because I'm still functioning on paper, but the distress is definitely there... it's just all trapped inside my own head.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to try to compensate or make up for it?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do any of that. I mean... I know some people do, you hear about that sort of thing on TV or whatever, but I've never made myself throw up. The very thought of it honestly just makes me shudder. I absolutely hate throwing up—even when I'm genuinely sick, like if I have a stomach bug, I will fight it for hours. So doing it on purpose? No way, I could never bring myself to do that. \n\nAnd laxatives, definitely not. I wouldn't even know where to start with that. Plus, honestly, my stomach is already in absolute knots the next day just from all the rich takeout and the sugar I ate, so adding something like a laxative into the mix just sounds like a complete nightmare. Especially with my work schedule! I have these morning Zoom calls with clients, and I have to be completely focused when I'm presenting a mock-up or a branding strategy. I can't be worried about running to the bathroom in the middle of pitching a design concept to a CEO. \n\nAs for exercise... I mean, I honestly wish I exercised normally, let alone excessively. But no, definitely not. Like I was saying earlier, I sit at my desk for ten, sometimes twelve hours a day, or I'm hunched over my laptop on the couch because my lower back is just completely shot from my terrible posture. By the time one of these eating things is over, I am just so physically exhausted and uncomfortably full. Like, painfully full, where your clothes feel too tight. The idea of putting on running shoes and trying to go for a jog at midnight? It's just not happening. I can barely muster the energy to waddle to the kitchen to throw the wrappers away. \n\nUsually, I just kind of sit there in this horrible food coma, feeling absolutely miserable and heavy. I don't try to undo it or burn it off. I just eventually drag myself to bed and just... lay there. I just sit with the guilt of it, honestly. It's just me, a terrible stomach ache, and a whole lot of regret. So, yeah, to answer your question, no, I don't do any of those things to make up for it. I just kind of suffer through the aftermath the next day.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. It's actually kind of shocking how fast it happens. I mean, I'll be sitting right there at my desk, right? Usually I have my dual monitors going, and I'll have all these Adobe Illustrator files open—maybe I'm tweaking a vector graphic or adjusting the kerning on a font for a logo, something really tedious where I'm just staring intently at the screen—and I'll have the food spread out all around my keyboard. \n\nAnd once I start, it's like my hands are just moving on complete autopilot. I eat so, so fast. It's not like a normal dinner where you take a bite, chew, take a sip of water, actually think about the flavor... no, I barely even notice the taste at all. I'm just blindly reaching and moving from one takeout container to the next. Like, from the carton of noodles to the dumplings to whatever else is sitting there, just rotating through them. \n\nAnd I'm just shoveling it in while staring at my screen. It's almost like a trance, you know? My eyes are glued to the Illustrator canvas, and my hand is just going back and forth, back and forth from the desk to my mouth. And then, I don't know, maybe just a few minutes will go by—literally just a handful of minutes—and I'll reach down for another bite and my fork will just scrape the bottom of the plastic container. And I'll look down, pull myself out of the screen for a second, and realize I've already swallowed almost all of it. \n\nIt's just... gone. A massive amount of food, just gone in a flash. It's honestly crazy how quickly I can put away that much food without even registering the physical experience of eating it. I don't savor it, I don't enjoy it, I just completely inhale it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes happen? Like, how many times a week or month would you say this occurs?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because I actually... I was looking at my phone just the other day, scrolling through my UberEats and DoorDash history. You know how those apps keep a record of literally everything? I was trying to figure out my budget, because honestly, the delivery fees and the service charges alone are just outrageous right now, and I felt like I was spending way too much money. \n\nAnd when I was looking back at all those delivery receipts, and kind of cross-referencing them with my work calendar—because my calendar is just this endless wall of deadlines and Zoom links—I noticed this really obvious, kind of terrifying pattern. There is usually at least one night every single week where an episode like this happens. Like, without fail. Sometimes it's more, maybe two times a week if I'm dealing with a particularly demanding client or if I have to do a massive revision on a logo design at the last minute and I'm up late. But definitely at least once a week. \n\nAnd as I kept scrolling back, and back... I realized this pattern has been there for several months now. I'd say it really started picking up when I took on this huge new corporate account a while back. The months just completely blur together when you work from home and stare at Adobe Illustrator all day, so I hadn't even realized how long it had been going on. \n\nBut yeah, seeing it all laid out there in my order history, all those late-night massive orders, week after week for months... it was a real wake-up call. Because when you're in the middle of it, you just tell yourself, 'Oh, it's just tonight, I'm just stressed today, it's a one-off.' But then you look at the app and it's every single week for months on end. You just don't realize how frequent it is until the data is literally just staring you right in the face, you know? It's just hard to look at.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you find yourself eating even after you feel full, until you're uncomfortably stuffed?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. It goes so far past just being full. I mean, normal fullness is when you finish a plate of food and think, 'Okay, that was good, I'm satisfied.' But this is completely different. I just keep going and going. Even when my brain is vaguely registering that I'm not hungry anymore, or that I've already had way too much, I just can't seem to stop until the containers are completely empty. \n\nAnd the physical sensation by the end of it... it's just awful. Like I mentioned before about feeling miserably heavy, it's honestly to the point where I am painfully, uncomfortably stuffed. By the end, I'm often so full that my stomach feels incredibly tight against my waistband. And I work from home, so I'm usually just wearing sweatpants or soft leggings, but even those elastic waistbands feel like they're cutting right into my skin because my stomach is just so distended from all the food. \n\nIt actually physically hurts. It gets so bad that I have to just sort of migrate over to the couch, and I literally have to sit very, very still. If I twist or bend or move too quickly, it's just this sharp, uncomfortable pressure, almost like I can't even take a full, deep breath. So I just stay frozen there on the cushions, sometimes for an hour or more, just waiting for the worst of that agonizing fullness to pass before I can even bend over to clean up the takeout bags and the mess off my desk. It's just a miserable, miserable physical feeling, but during the actual eating part, I just keep pushing right past it without even stopping to realize how much it's going to hurt.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Usually I have my dual monitors going, and I'll have all these Adobe Illustrator files open—maybe I'm tweaking a vector graphic or adjusting the kerning on a font for a logo, something really tedious where I'm just staring intently at the screen—and I'll have the food spread out all around my keyboard. \n\nAnd once I start, it's like my hands are just moving on complete autopilot. I eat so, so fast. It's not like a normal dinner where you take a bite, chew, take a sip of water, actually think about the flavor... no, I barely even notice the taste at all. I'm just blindly reaching and moving from one takeout container to the next. Like, from the carton of noodles to the dumplings to whatever else is sitting there, just rotating through them. \n\nAnd I'm just shoveling it in while staring at my screen. It's almost like a trance, you know? My eyes are glued to the Illustrator canvas, and my hand is just going back and forth, back and forth from the desk to my mouth. And then, I don't know, maybe just a few minutes will go by—literally just a handful of minutes—and I'll reach down for another bite and my fork will just scrape the bottom of the plastic container. And I'll look down, pull myself out of the screen for a second, and realize I've already swallowed almost all of it. \n\nIt's just... gone. A massive amount of food, just gone in a flash. It's honestly crazy how quickly I can put away that much food without even registering the physical experience of eating it. I don't savor it, I don't enjoy it, I just completely inhale it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes happen? Like, how many times a week or month would you say this occurs?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because I actually... I was looking at my phone just the other day, scrolling through my UberEats and DoorDash history. You know how those apps keep a record of literally everything? I was trying to figure out my budget, because honestly, the delivery fees and the service charges alone are just outrageous right now, and I felt like I was spending way too much money. \n\nAnd when I was looking back at all those delivery receipts, and kind of cross-referencing them with my work calendar—because my calendar is just this endless wall of deadlines and Zoom links—I noticed this really obvious, kind of terrifying pattern. There is usually at least one night every single week where an episode like this happens. Like, without fail. Sometimes it's more, maybe two times a week if I'm dealing with a particularly demanding client or if I have to do a massive revision on a logo design at the last minute and I'm up late. But definitely at least once a week. \n\nAnd as I kept scrolling back, and back... I realized this pattern has been there for several months now. I'd say it really started picking up when I took on this huge new corporate account a while back. The months just completely blur together when you work from home and stare at Adobe Illustrator all day, so I hadn't even realized how long it had been going on. \n\nBut yeah, seeing it all laid out there in my order history, all those late-night massive orders, week after week for months... it was a real wake-up call. Because when you're in the middle of it, you just tell yourself, 'Oh, it's just tonight, I'm just stressed today, it's a one-off.' But then you look at the app and it's every single week for months on end. You just don't realize how frequent it is until the data is literally just staring you right in the face, you know? It's just hard to look at.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you find yourself eating even after you feel full, until you're uncomfortably stuffed?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. It goes so far past just being full. I mean, normal fullness is when you finish a plate of food and think, 'Okay, that was good, I'm satisfied.' But this is completely different. I just keep going and going. Even when my brain is vaguely registering that I'm not hungry anymore, or that I've already had way too much, I just can't seem to stop until the containers are completely empty. \n\nAnd the physical sensation by the end of it... it's just awful. Like I mentioned before about feeling miserably heavy, it's honestly to the point where I am painfully, uncomfortably stuffed. By the end, I'm often so full that my stomach feels incredibly tight against my waistband. And I work from home, so I'm usually just wearing sweatpants or soft leggings, but even those elastic waistbands feel like they're cutting right into my skin because my stomach is just so distended from all the food. \n\nIt actually physically hurts. It gets so bad that I have to just sort of migrate over to the couch, and I literally have to sit very, very still. If I twist or bend or move too quickly, it's just this sharp, uncomfortable pressure, almost like I can't even take a full, deep breath. So I just stay frozen there on the cushions, sometimes for an hour or more, just waiting for the worst of that agonizing fullness to pass before I can even bend over to clean up the takeout bags and the mess off my desk. It's just a miserable, miserable physical feeling, but during the actual eating part, I just keep pushing right past it without even stopping to realize how much it's going to hurt.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you recall if these episodes typically happen within a short, discrete period of time—like within a couple of hours—or do they tend to stretch out over a longer part of the evening?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s definitely a short period. I mean, it’s not like I’m grazing all evening, you know? It’s very concentrated. Like I was saying, I’ll be working late, and I’ll decide to order dinner. And in my head, I’m telling myself, "Okay, I’m just getting dinner because I’m working through the evening and I need sustenance." 

But then... it’s not just dinner. I’ll order a main entrée, right? But then I’ll add on a bunch of sides, like maybe an order of egg rolls and some crab rangoon, and then I’ll throw in a dessert because the delivery app always prompts you with those pictures of chocolate cake or whatever right before you check out. And then, while I’m waiting for the delivery to arrive, I might even go to the kitchen and grab some chips or a sleeve of cookies just to have right there on the desk. 

And once it all arrives and I start eating, it all happens so fast. It’s definitely within a couple of hours, usually way less than that. Like I said, I’m just inhaling it. And it’s just a massive, massive amount of food. I mean, it’s way, way more than I would ever eat if I were out at a restaurant with friends, or if my sister was over, or if literally anyone were sitting across from me. If someone else were there, I’d be so embarrassed to have all those containers open in front of me. But because I’m alone, and it’s just me and my monitors... I just eat all of it, all at once, in this one intense, short window of time.

And I work from home, so I'm usually just wearing sweatpants or soft leggings, but even those elastic waistbands feel like they're cutting right into my skin because my stomach is just so distended from all the food. \n\nIt actually physically hurts. It gets so bad that I have to just sort of migrate over to the couch, and I literally have to sit very, very still. If I twist or bend or move too quickly, it's just this sharp, uncomfortable pressure, almost like I can't even take a full, deep breath. So I just stay frozen there on the cushions, sometimes for an hour or more, just waiting for the worst of that agonizing fullness to pass before I can even bend over to clean up the takeout bags and the mess off my desk. It's just a miserable, miserable physical feeling, but during the actual eating part, I just keep pushing right past it without even stopping to realize how much it's going to hurt.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if these episodes typically happen within a short, discrete period of time—like within a couple of hours—or do they tend to stretch out over a longer part of the evening?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a short period. I mean, it’s not like I’m grazing all evening, you know? It’s very concentrated. Like I was saying, I’ll be working late, and I’ll decide to order dinner. And in my head, I’m telling myself, \"Okay, I’m just getting dinner because I’m working through the evening and I need sustenance.\" \n\nBut then... it’s not just dinner. I’ll order a main entrée, right? But then I’ll add on a bunch of sides, like maybe an order of egg rolls and some crab rangoon, and then I’ll throw in a dessert because the delivery app always prompts you with those pictures of chocolate cake or whatever right before you check out. And then, while I’m waiting for the delivery to arrive, I might even go to the kitchen and grab some chips or a sleeve of cookies just to have right there on the desk. \n\nAnd once it all arrives and I start eating, it all happens so fast. It’s definitely within a couple of hours, usually way less than that. Like I said, I’m just inhaling it. And it’s just a massive, massive amount of food. I mean, it’s way, way more than I would ever eat if I were out at a restaurant with friends, or if my sister was over, or if literally anyone were sitting across from me. If someone else were there, I’d be so embarrassed to have all those containers open in front of me. But because I’m alone, and it’s just me and my monitors... I just eat all of it, all at once, in this one intense, short window of time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. I mean, I kind of touched on that just a second ago, but it goes so much deeper than just not wanting my sister to see. It’s like I live these two completely different lives when it comes to food. If I’m out with coworkers—like, sometimes the design team will go out for lunch, or we’ll grab drinks and appetizers after a big campaign launch—I am so, so careful. I make a very conscious effort to eat lightly. I’ll just order a side salad with the dressing on the side, or I’ll just pick at a flatbread and make a big show of saying how full I am, and I'll take half of it home in a box. I want everyone to think I just have this tiny appetite, you know? I’d never, ever let my friends or colleagues see me eat a large portion of anything.

But then, when I’m actually alone... it’s a totally different story. When I’m back in my condo and it’s after midnight, and I know for an absolute fact that no one is going to drop by, that's when it happens. And the crazy thing is, even though I live alone and my door is locked, I still get so incredibly paranoid. Before I even open the delivery bags, I literally walk through the living room and make sure all the blinds are pulled tightly shut. I know I’m up on the third floor and nobody is realistically looking into my windows at 1 AM, but I just have this overwhelming, irrational fear that someone might somehow see me sitting there surrounded by all that food. 

And then the cleanup is a whole production, too. As soon as the episode is over and the physical pain subsides enough for me to stand up, I have to get rid of the delivery containers immediately. I can't just leave them sitting in the kitchen trash. I'll crush all the plastic boxes and shove the grease-stained paper bags deep down into a garbage bag, and I'll usually take it straight out to the complex dumpster right then and there in the middle of the night. I just have to get the evidence out of my space quickly, because I would be absolutely mortified—I mean, truly, I can't even express how ashamed I'd be—if anyone, like the maintenance guy or a friend stopping by the next morning, saw the sheer volume of containers in my trash. The idea of anyone witnessing it or finding out how much there actually was is just completely terrifying to me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, God, yes. The guilt is... it's honestly suffocating. It doesn't even matter that nobody else is there to see it. It's almost worse because I'm the only one who knows, and I'm just sitting there with myself, dealing with the aftermath. I mean, the second the episode is over, and I’m just sitting there on the couch, unable to move like I told you, my brain just starts attacking me. 

I just start thinking about how pathetic it is. I'm 33 years old, I have a good job, I have my own place, I have a degree, you know? I should have my life together. I should be able to control myself. But then I look at the empty containers on the desk, the wrappers, the crumbs everywhere, and I just feel this overwhelming wave of disgust. I literally look at my own hands and feel disgusted by them. I think, 'How could you do this again? You promised yourself yesterday you wouldn't do this.' 

And the self-hatred... it's just so intense. I'll sit there and just berate myself for hours. I'll tell myself I'm gross, that I have no willpower, that I'm just this weak person who can't even manage a basic human function like eating without turning it into this massive, destructive event. I mean, I'll even cry sometimes. Just sit there, clutching my stomach because it hurts so much, and cry because I feel so incredibly ashamed of what I just did. And the guilt doesn't just go away the next morning, either. It lingers. It hangs over me the entire next day while I'm trying to work or design something, and all I can think about is how disgusting I was the night before. It's just this constant, heavy cycle of doing it, and then hating myself so, so much for doing it, but knowing I'll probably just end up doing it again.

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

{
  "candidates": ["F50.8", "F50.9"],
  "note": "Binge Eating Disorder (F50.8) remains a strong candidate given the recurrent episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time with a sense of loss of control, significant distress, and associated shame and guilt. The absence of compensatory behaviors rules out Bulimia Nervosa (F50.2). The distinction between F50.8 and F50.9 (Unspecified Feeding or Eating Disorder) hinges on whether the full criteria for BED are met, which is increasingly likely based on frequency (at least once weekly for several months), but may still require clarification.",
  "is_final": false

I end up working from home a lot, pushing up against these really tight deadlines, staring at my screens for hours on end. \n\nAnd what brings me in is that on these late work nights, I’ll open up a delivery app and order what I tell myself is just going to be a normal dinner. I just can't seem to hit the brakes once that momentum starts, no matter how much I'm begging myself internally to just put the food down. I will be sitting there, and my brain is screaming at me to just close the takeout box, or put the bag of cookies away, or whatever it is I am eating. And I just... don't. I'll wake up and walk into the living room, and I'll see the empty takeout containers still sitting on my coffee table, or the cookie wrappers stuffed into the top of the trash can, and I just feel so... embarrassed. I'll just stand there making my morning coffee, staring at the mess I made, and I will just beat myself up over it for hours. I'll be thinking, 'You're thirty-three years old, you manage million-dollar campaigns, you have a degree, why can't you just eat a normal dinner like a normal, functioning adult?' \n\nSo it's not like I'm missing work or blowing off social events, because, like I said, I'm doing this alone in my apartment late at night. But it's this internal monologue that just completely tears me down. I spend so much mental energy just being mad at myself and feeling ashamed. And then I have to put on this professional, smiling face for my client calls and act like I have my entire life perfectly put together, when really I'm just so disappointed in myself for what I did the night before. So I don't know if that's exactly what you mean by interfering, because I'm still functioning on paper, but the distress is definitely there... it's just all trapped inside my own head.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time, do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives, or exercise excessively to try to compensate or make up for it?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do any of that. I mean... I know some people do, you hear about that sort of thing on TV or whatever, but I've never made myself throw up. The very thought of it honestly just makes me shudder. I absolutely hate throwing up—even when I'm genuinely sick, like if I have a stomach bug, I will fight it for hours. So doing it on purpose? No way, I could never bring myself to do that. \n\nAnd laxatives, definitely not. I wouldn't even know where to start with that. Plus, honestly, my stomach is already in absolute knots the next day just from all the rich takeout and the sugar I ate, so adding something like a laxative into the mix just sounds like a complete nightmare. It goes so far past just being full. I mean, normal fullness is when you finish a plate of food and think, 'Okay, that was good, I'm satisfied.' But this is completely different. I just keep going and going. Even when my brain is vaguely registering that I'm not hungry anymore, or that I've already had way too much, I just can't seem to stop until the containers are completely empty. \n\nAnd the physical sensation by the end of it... it's just awful. If I’m out with coworkers—like, sometimes the design team will go out for lunch, or we’ll grab drinks and appetizers after a big campaign launch—I am so, so careful. I make a very conscious effort to eat lightly. I’ll just order a side salad with the dressing on the side, or I’ll just pick at a flatbread and make a big show of saying how full I am, and I'll take half of it home in a box. I want everyone to think I just have this tiny appetite, you know? I’d never, ever let my friends or colleagues see me eat a large portion of anything.\n\nBut then, when I’m actually alone... it’s a totally different story. Before I even open the delivery bags, I literally walk through the living room and make sure all the blinds are pulled tightly shut. I know I’m up on the third floor and nobody is realistically looking into my windows at 1 AM, but I just have this overwhelming, irrational fear that someone might somehow see me sitting there surrounded by all that food. \n\nAnd then the cleanup is a whole production, too.
