I can't just leave them sitting in the kitchen trash. I'll crush all the plastic boxes and shove the grease-stained paper bags deep down into a garbage bag, and I'll usually take it straight out to the complex dumpster right then and there in the middle of the night. I just have to get the evidence out of my space quickly, because I would be absolutely mortified—I mean, truly, I can't even express how ashamed I'd be—if anyone, like the maintenance guy or a friend stopping by the next morning, saw the sheer volume of containers in my trash. The idea of anyone witnessing it or finding out how much there actually was is just completely terrifying to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel intense embarrassment, guilt, or self-disgust specifically about these eating episodes, even if no one else sees them?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. The guilt is... it's honestly suffocating. It doesn't even matter that nobody else is there to see it. It's almost worse because I'm the only one who knows, and I'm just sitting there with myself, dealing with the aftermath. I mean, the second the episode is over, and I’m just sitting there on the couch, unable to move like I told you, my brain just starts attacking me. \n\nI just start thinking about how pathetic it is. I'm 33 years old, I have a good job, I have my own place, I have a degree, you know? I should have my life together. I should be able to control myself. But then I look at the empty containers on the desk, the wrappers, the crumbs everywhere, and I just feel this overwhelming wave of disgust. I literally look at my own hands and feel disgusted by them. I think, 'How could you do this again? You promised yourself yesterday you wouldn't do this.' \n\nAnd the self-hatred... it's just so intense. I'll sit there and just berate myself for hours. I'll tell myself I'm gross, that I have no willpower, that I'm just this weak person who can't even manage a basic human function like eating without turning it into this massive, destructive event. I mean, I'll even cry sometimes. Just sit there, clutching my stomach because it hurts so much, and cry because I feel so incredibly ashamed of what I just did. And the guilt doesn't just go away the next morning, either. It lingers. It hangs over me the entire next day while I'm trying to work or design something, and all I can think about is how disgusting I was the night before. I make a very conscious effort to eat lightly. I’ll just order a side salad with the dressing on the side, or I’ll just pick at a flatbread and make a big show of saying how full I am, and I'll take half of it home in a box. I want everyone to think I just have this tiny appetite, you know? I’d never, ever let my friends or colleagues see me eat a large portion of anything.\n\nBut then, when I’m actually alone... it’s a totally different story. When I’m back in my condo and it’s after midnight, and I know for an absolute fact that no one is going to drop by, that's when it happens. And the crazy thing is, even though I live alone and my door is locked, I still get so incredibly paranoid. Before I even open the delivery bags, I literally walk through the living room and make sure all the blinds are pulled tightly shut. I know I’m up on the third floor and nobody is realistically looking into my windows at 1 AM, but I just have this overwhelming, irrational fear that someone might somehow see me sitting there surrounded by all that food. \n\nAnd then the cleanup is a whole production, too. I can't just leave them sitting in the kitchen trash. I just have to get the evidence out of my space quickly, because I would be absolutely mortified—I mean, truly, I can't even express how ashamed I'd be—if anyone, like the maintenance guy or a friend stopping by the next morning, saw the sheer volume of containers in my trash. The idea of anyone witnessing it or finding out how much there actually was is just completely terrifying to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel intense embarrassment, guilt, or self-disgust specifically about these eating episodes, even if no one else sees them?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. The guilt is... it's honestly suffocating. It doesn't even matter that nobody else is there to see it. It's almost worse because I'm the only one who knows, and I'm just sitting there with myself, dealing with the aftermath. I mean, the second the episode is over, and I’m just sitting there on the couch, unable to move like I told you, my brain just starts attacking me. \n\nI just start thinking about how pathetic it is. I'm 33 years old, I have a good job, I have my own place, I have a degree, you know? I should have my life together. I should be able to control myself. But then I look at the empty containers on the desk, the wrappers, the crumbs everywhere, and I just feel this overwhelming wave of disgust. I literally look at my own hands and feel disgusted by them. I think, 'How could you do this again? You promised yourself yesterday you wouldn't do this.' \n\nAnd the self-hatred... it's just so intense. I'll sit there and just berate myself for hours. I'll tell myself I'm gross, that I have no willpower, that I'm just this weak person who can't even manage a basic human function like eating without turning it into this massive, destructive event. I mean, I'll even cry sometimes. Just sit there, clutching my stomach because it hurts so much, and cry because I feel so incredibly ashamed of what I just did. And the guilt doesn't just go away the next morning, either. It lingers. It hangs over me the entire next day while I'm trying to work or design something, and all I can think about is how disgusting I was the night before. It's just this constant, heavy cycle of doing it, and then hating myself so, so much for doing it, but knowing I'll probably just end up doing it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong sense of shame or distress specifically about the fact that you engage in these eating episodes, even when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... that's exactly what I was just trying to explain. It's... yes, absolutely. The shame is honestly the biggest part of it.
